Crypto
Investing
for Beginners

A Comprehensive Beginner’s
Guide to Learn the Realms of Crypto Investing from A-Z and Make Money from Crypto Assets

 

 

 

James Bruwer

Introduction

The huge success of Bitcoin and Dogecoin over the last few months, particularly with how their market prices have skyrocketed and made millionaires out of so many people seemingly overnight, have further fueled the frenzy over cryptocurrencies in general. Over the last few years, cryptocurrencies have become known as the modern, digital versions of the California gold rush of yesteryears.

Still, people are divided over-investing in this relatively new breed of financial assets. Some look at these as nothing more than a cool way of gambling. On the other end, some people look at them as the way by which they can accomplish their dreams of grand riches and early retirement.

So, what are cryptocurrencies? Are they the cool younger sibling of the lottery, or is it a dependable asset upon which we can build solid financial futures?

Well, I’d say it’s neither of the two extremes. The real essence of cryptocurrencies lies somewhere in the middle, neither a pure gamble nor a solid financial investment. Where in the middle does the real substance lie?

Well, that’s what this book is for.

As you read through these pages, you’ll get a clear idea of what cryptocurrencies are. More importantly, you’ll learn how to invest wisely in this new investment species so that you can maximize your chances of accomplishing your financial goals. This includes identifying low and high-risk cryptos as well as potential investment scams.

If you’re ready to learn how to successfully surf this new investment wave, turn the page and let’s begin.

Chapter 1

 

Cryptocurrency Basics

Today’s frenzy over Bitcoin, Dogecoin, and all other cryptocurrencies is both new and old. Strictly speaking, it’s a new craze because this type of financial asset is a relatively new one. Unlike stocks, bonds, and government securities that have been around for many decades, digital currencies have only been around for a little over ten years. The oldest altcoin, i.e., cryptocurrency not named Bitcoin, is just ten years old as of 2021.

But despite the asset being relatively new, the craze surrounding it isn’t. There have been several notable manias similar to this. These include:

  1. The Holland tulip craze in the 1600s.
  2. The California gold rush in the 1800s.
  3. The 1990s dot-com mania.

We can compare the current frenzy over digital coins to manias like these because it shares common characteristics. One of them is novelty.

In the Holland tulip craze, the public went crazy over the rare flower after they entered the mainstream flower market. In the California gold rush, it was the promise of rapid great wealth through mining gold in an area that most people didn’t know was abundant in the metal. And with the dot-com mania, the novelty lay in the out-of-this-world accelerated growth on the Internet-related niche of the tech industry, which was a quickly flourishing new technology that had the potential to change the way the world communicates, transacts, and entertains. Looking at where we are now, it’s not hard to see that the Internet has ushered in the 4th industrial revolution that has greatly changed the way people live, communicate, and are entertained.

This is a normal part of human psychology. Think about it, don’t you get excited with the prospect of getting your new smartphone or going on a trip to a place you haven’t been to before? New things get people excited all the time. And if that new thing has the potential to make you very rich quickly, the initial excitement can mutate into a frenzy.

Another reason why it’s similar to the three abovementioned manias is the strong belief in guaranteed riches in a short period. Given its potential to change the way financial transactions are done, no wonder cryptocurrencies are coveted by millions of investors worldwide.

The third similar characteristic cryptocurrencies share with tulips, gold, and Internet companies are the real stories of people who became millionaires almost overnight by investing in digital currencies. Given the voracious appetite many speculative investors already have for digital coins, well-publicized testimonies of rapid riches we’re like gasoline poured into an open flame. It fueled the fire frenzy over this asset, and given their limited nature, the huge spike in demand has pushed their market prices way above investor expectations. Thus, a vicious upward spiral continues where soaring prices make more people want to buy digital coins, which puts more upward pressure on market prices.

Cryptocurrencies: What Are They?

To clearly understand the nature of this type of financial asset, let’s do a little etymology on the term.

The term cryptocurrency is the union of two words: crypto and currency. Crypto is a shorthand term for cryptography, which refers to the practice or science of message encoding and decoding. This process uses cipher codes to prevent unauthorized parties from understanding messages that were meant to be secret.

Many of today’s most popular websites or applications use cryptography to ensure information security. By encrypting and decrypting data and information sent and received over the Internet, hackers won’t be able to understand and benefit from them.

Let’s look at two contradictory examples of cryptography. The first example is three of the most popular messaging apps: WhatsApp, Viber, and Telegram. These feature end-to-end encryption, or E2EE as it is popularly known. This means the app on your phone or computer encrypts the messages you send to your friends and the same app installed on theirs decodes them. If, by chance, hackers can intercept your messages, you don’t have to worry because, without the encryption key used by your app, they won’t be able to decode them.

Being encrypted obviously means that the asset is in digital form. As such, cryptocurrencies are also referred to as digital assets that have no physical or printed counterparts.

This is one of the main differences between cryptos and the funds you have in your bank or fund storage platforms like PayPal. Whether you store dollars or other foreign currencies in such accounts, they all have physical counterparts that you can withdraw for non-digital transactions.

Because cryptocurrencies are purely digital in nature, you cannot make physical withdrawals for buying stuff from vendors that don’t have digital payment facilities like your favorite hotdog stand in the corner. You can only use them to buy from vendors that specifically accept certain types of cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin or Ethereum.

As for the 2nd word in the term, I don’t think it needs any explanation. Currency refers to, well, money or currencies.

Hence, you can simply think of cryptocurrencies as digitally encrypted money.

The Blockchain

All business transactions are recorded by companies in their books of accounts or accounting ledgers. Take, for example, when you buy something online using your credit card or bank account.

The number of your purchase is recorded as money owed in your account with your credit card issuer. If you use a bank fund transfer to do the same, your bank account balance would be deducted by the same amount.

Similarly, when you pay your credit card bills, the money owed under your credit card account will be deducted by the same amount. And if, by chance, you get a refund for a previous purchase funded by a bank transfer, the vendor will send the money back to you through your bank account. The amount in your balance will increase by the appropriate transaction value refunded.

It’s no different with cryptocurrency transactions. If you decide to buy a particular cryptocurrency, your account balance and all succeeding transactions will be recorded in its digital master ledger or file. As with a business’s books of accounts or ledgers, your cryptocurrency accounts will contain all your transactions with said currency from the very beginning.

However, that’s where the similarities end. Businesses record their financial transactions and current balances in their accounting books. For cryptocurrencies, these are recorded in a master ledger or master file called the blockchain.

Pardon the use of etymology again, but the blockchain is made up of the words block and chain. A validated cryptocurrency transaction is referred to as a block. Because a master ledger or a master file comprises all validated transactions for a specific cryptocurrency, this Masterfile may be considered as a chain of validated transactions. Hence, the term blockchain.

Key Characteristics of Cryptocurrencies

Aside from being purely digital in nature, one important characteristic of cryptocurrencies is decentralization. In layman’s terms, it means that the master file or ledger of all validated transactions for specific crypto isn’t kept or maintained by just one party but by all parties that have ever transacted it.

To better appreciate this, consider your bank records. The sole custodian of all transactions with your bank, including the transactions of others with the same bank, is the bank itself. If hackers want to steal your money and those of the bank’s other depositors, they just need to hack one database: the bank’s.

Contrast this to a cryptocurrency’s blockchain. Because a blockchain is a master ledger or master file of all validated transactions distributed among all parties that have and continue to transact it, hackers need to break into all the records of everybody involved. In other words, they’ll need to break into each copy of the blockchain that many people own to hack and steal successfully.

This is the primary reason why blockchain technology is being hailed as a revolutionary data security invention. The sheer number of records that sinister elements need to hack makes it practically impossible to steal your cryptocurrencies.

Another very important feature of cryptocurrencies is autonomy. This means freedom from all forms of government monitoring and regulation. Regular or fiat currencies such as the US dollar, Japanese yen, or Philippine peso are regulated and controlled by their respective sovereign authorities’ monetary agencies. What’s the implication of this?

First, the market supply of Fiat currencies is completely controlled by their sovereign governments. This is a good thing because one of the most powerful monetary policy tools for managing inflation and economic growth is controlling the money supply. But more importantly, sovereign governments have the authority to print new money in the form of their local currencies.

With cryptocurrencies, there are no such regulatory bodies. This is because they are not official legal tenders, and private enterprises create them instead of governments. Thus, cryptocurrency transactions and their market supplies are beyond the reach of regulatory authorities.

Another key characteristic of cryptocurrencies that’s related to their autonomy is a limited supply. Governments can easily mint more of their local currency when the need arises, but that is not the case with digital coins like Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Dogecoin. This is because the number of units for each cryptocurrency we’re already determined before their public launches and are already cast in stone. And because it’s purely digital in nature, they have embedded codes or protocols that make supply-increasing alterations or modifications impossible.

Just to clarify, it doesn’t mean new units of cryptocurrencies can’t be created or issued to the public. When a cryptocurrency is launched, only a portion of its total supply is released to the public. Otherwise, there would be no incentive for people to mine for new units by validating transactions.

At this point, you may have been confused by the last paragraph. Allow me to explain the concept of mining in more detail.

As mentioned earlier, a cryptocurrency blockchain is decentralized across all its users. This also means that unlike banks and other financial institutions, no one is particularly responsible for processing and validating transactions involving a specific digital token.

If this is the case, how are cryptocurrency transactions facilitated and, more importantly, validated? This is where individuals who are called “miners” come into the picture.

Miners use their own computers to process and validate transactions in exchange for new units of a specific cryptocurrency. Just like how gold and other precious metals are extracted from the earth, these individuals use dedicated terminals to validate transactions and “mine” for new units of digital tokens.

To help you get a clearer picture of this, let’s say you bought 1 unit of Bitcoin. Before that unit is officially lodged under your account in the blockchain, it must be validated first. How does the validation process go?

Bitcoin’s system will generate algorithms or mathematical equations that are unique for every transaction, including yours. Only when somebody cracks open or solves the specific algorithm or mathematical equation assigned to your transaction can your transaction be validated and officially recorded in the blockchain.

But since the Bitcoin system itself doesn’t process or validate transactions, it relies on countless numbers of individuals who are willing to “lend” their computers’ spare computing powers to solve these equations. This process entails costs, whether it’s investing in additional computer equipment, or the cost of electricity used to power their terminals. Hence, miners need to have an incentive to commit to solving generated algorithms to validate Bitcoin transactions.

Why Cryptos?

The primary reason why digital tokens were created is to provide alternative payment methods to consumers. However, its autonomy proved to be a hindrance to accomplishing this purpose, together with the fact that it’s a completely new payment concept with no proven track records for either safety or efficiency. Hence, it has – and continues to until today – struggled to gain mainstream acceptance as an alternative medium for business transactions.

But compared to the early days, the number of vendors that accept them – particularly Bitcoin – as payment options are significantly greater in number now. Some of the biggest retailers accepting Bitcoin as payments include Microsoft and online selling giant Overstock.

And it’s not just retailers that are jumping on the cryptocurrency bandwagon. Some banks reportedly allow withdrawing the fiat currency equivalent of cryptocurrencies on their ATMs.

As more vendors start accepting cryptocurrency payments, this type of financial asset’s popularity continues to grow along with their market prices. And it’s in this area that cryptocurrencies have become immensely popular, making its original “mission” take a backseat for the meantime.

As one of the world’s hottest, if not the hottest, financial assets today, its current success as an investment outlet appears to dwarf the slow but steady progress of being accepted as a mainstream payment method alternative. Proof of this is trading Bitcoin futures and options contracts on the Chicago Mercantile Exchange (CME) and Chicago Board Options Exchange (CBOE), respectively. These are two of the biggest financial exchanges in the US and in the world and being traded on these platforms only continues to validate the current rock star status of cryptocurrencies.

Chapter 2

 

Why Invest in Cryptocurrencies?

Just because many have – and continue to – enjoy out-of-this-world returns on their digital currency investments doesn’t mean that buying them can give you the same results. While this investment genre is as lucrative as any investment asset can get, it comes with a caveat: high risk.

Volatility

As an investor, one principle you should know by heart is the risk-return principle, i.e., the higher your expected returns, the higher your investment risks.

In simple terms, you must be willing to take higher financial risks to get the chance for potentially higher returns. As professional traders would say: When it comes to enjoying higher-than-average returns on investment, volatility should be your best friend.

What does volatility mean? It refers to how big or small the market price swings of your investment asset are. The bigger the price swings are, the higher the volatility and vice-versa. What’s the implication?

If you choose to invest in an asset whose market price swings are narrow or small, your potential return on investment will likely be average or low. If you invest in an asset whose market price swings are historically great, your chances of getting a high ROI are much greater.

Again, you should be comfortable taking on higher financial risks if you want to enjoy much higher returns. If you’re not comfortable with this, cryptocurrencies aren’t an ideal investment for you. Why?

Consider that in December 2017, the price of Bitcoin skyrocketed by more than 400%. But on one trading day in May 2021, its price dropped by more than 30% in a matter of hours before recovering slightly.

That’s why you must settle in your heart to accept the high investment risk that’s associated with cryptocurrencies before taking the investment plunge. If you do, the chances of you getting rewarded very handsomely are high.

New Kid on the Investment Block

One of the reasons for the generally high market volatility of cryptocurrency prices is its status as the newest investment asset class. As mentioned earlier, the oldest cryptos not named Bitcoin are only ten years old. As an investment genre, this is still very young.

If you’re fine with taking on higher investment risks, then the asset class’ youth is one reason for considering investing in digital coins. Why?

Consider stock investments. Two of the most used classifications are blue chips and growth stocks. The former refers to stocks of companies that have been around for ages, have the biggest market capitalizations on their respective exchanges, and are typically in mature but steady industries. While maturity and financial stability make for safer investments, growth potential has likely plateaued. This means returns on investments in the company have also plateaued.

On the other hand, growth stocks refer to shares of stocks of relatively young companies with a lot of growth potential. But with growth potential comes greater uncertainty, especially for companies in young industries. And the greater the uncertainty, the greater the risk – and potentially the higher the returns.

Considering that Bitcoin – the pioneer cryptocurrency – was only created in 2008 and was first transacted in 2009, digital coins are a very young investment asset class. As a result, it has much greater financial risk compared to traditional investment classes, including stocks. Thus, it also provides opportunities for much higher returns on investments.

Another advantage of young investment classes with high growth potential is that investors are typically positive and excited about them. Coupled with the out-of-this-world return on Bitcoin investments, cryptocurrencies, in general, enjoy a very positive outlook among investors.

Privacy

Another reason why many investors choose cryptocurrencies is privacy. If you remember, this type of asset class enjoys complete autonomy from government financial regulators. Also, blockchain ledgers don’t reveal the names of their investors. Instead of account names or email addresses, they use specific wallet addresses. As a result, other people have no way of telling who owns the accounts. Talk about the ultimate in privacy.

This cryptocurrency advantage can be a double-edged sword. It’s because there’s no distinction as to who can enjoy this level of investment privacy. Whether the investor is an upright law-abiding citizen or a money-laundering drug lord, he or she enjoys the benefit of complete privacy. This is one of the reasons why cryptocurrencies, in general, get a lot of flak from authorities. Money laundering criminals can successfully hide the fruits of their criminal labor through digital coins.

However, the high level of privacy cryptocurrencies offer can only benefit you as an investor regardless of your status with the law. If you’re a law-abiding citizen, just think of it as a way of ensuring that some of your investments are safe from the prying eyes of people who may have nefarious plans against you. These may include people from the government as well.

Not a Perfect Investment

While it’s true that cryptos provide plenty of investment benefits to warrant serious consideration from investors like you, it’s not perfect. Just like any other investment vehicle, it has its share of disadvantages, too. Let’s look at them in more detail in the next chapter.

Chapter 3

 

Disadvantages of
Cryptocurrency Investing

When it comes to investments, it’s important to have a positive mindset. However, you need to be careful not to go overboard and become completely oblivious to risks and disadvantages. Don’t think that considering potential negative outcomes and risks make you a pessimist. To maximize your chances of investing successfully in cryptocurrencies or any other financial assets, it’s best to keep yourself as informed as possible. This includes being aware of the disadvantages associated with cryptocurrency investing.

Volatility

Surprise, surprise! One of the primary advantages of digital coins can also be one of its biggest disadvantages. How will you know if volatility is an advantage or disadvantage for you? The answer lies in your risk tolerance.

If you’re the kind of person who is more excited and energized with high volatility, then it can be your best friend. But if you’re the type that gets easily rattled when the market prices of your investments drop significantly, volatility may be more of a disadvantage for you.

Keep in mind that large price swings, especially huge drops, can put you so deep in the red fast. Consider the intraday 30% price drop of Bitcoin on May 18, 2021.

Imagine if you had invested $1000 in it the day before. You were so excited at the prospect of your investment growing by at least 10% in the next few days or weeks.

Then within a few hours of trading the next day, the price of Bitcoin plunges by as much as 30%. This means the investment you made yesterday has shrunk by $300 to just $700 overnight.

If you’re the type that easily panics, can you imagine the emotional distress of seeing the value of your investment plunge by nearly a third in just a few short hours? What if the money you invested was meant to pay for a scheduled major expense a few weeks from now, and you just thought of making it earn some money while waiting? Can you imagine the anxiety that’s such volatility can cause you?

Volatility is one of the major reasons why cryptocurrency investing is not for everyone. To successfully ride the wave, you need to be calm and collected during periods of market volatility, such as the May 18, 2021, trading day. Just like with market-driven financial assets like cryptocurrencies and stocks, market price drops don’t necessarily translate to actual losses.

If you have enough patience and hold on until prices eventually recover, you’ll only experience what is called paper loss. Also referred to as marked to market losses, this type of loss is merely a theoretical one. Paper or mark to market losses only tells you how much money you stand to lose if you decide to liquidate your investments at current market prices.

Going back to our theoretical example, your $300 paper loss due to the 30% price plunge of Bitcoin simply means you lose $300 if you sell at current market prices. But if you decide to wait for the market out, you won’t suffer any financial losses. Holding on will also allow you to let your investment return thanks to its acquisition price or even go higher. Then, you can sell at either break even or a profit.

But if you feel volatility isn’t your thing, that’s perfectly alright. Other less volatile investments in the market may be better suited for you. These include blue-chip stocks, AAA-rated bonds, and US treasuries.

But if you want to give cryptocurrency investing a try despite your low-risk tolerance, there’s a workaround to that. To ensure you won’t panic or suffer anxiety attacks in the event of a major price plunge, I suggest investing an amount that you’re perfectly comfortable losing.

You may be wondering why bother investing if you think you will just lose your entire investment? The point of limiting your investment to an amount you have no problems losing doesn’t mean you are expecting a sure investment loss. Rather, limiting your investment to such an amount will not make you worry and keep you from liquidating your investment prematurely when the market price moves unfavorably. More importantly, you will have enough peace of mind to learn to be a more objective investor when it comes to risky assets like cryptos.

Another variation of this workaround is investing an amount of money that you won’t be needing anytime soon. Why?

Referring again to our Bitcoin example, what would happen if your $1000 investment was meant to pay for your rent next month and you see its value plunge to just $700.00 overnight? Worst, what if that was your only available money for rental payments?

By investing an amount of money that’s not earmarked for any major expense, you’ll feel more at peace taking a long-term investment approach. Should your cryptocurrency investments encounter rough patches, like the May 18, 2021 trading day for Bitcoin, you won’t be anxious because you know you have enough time to wait for market prices to recover.

Uncertainty

Another double-edged characteristic of cryptocurrencies is their young age. Earlier, we talked about how being a young and exciting breed of investments likens it to growth stocks, i.e., they have high growth potential and, consequently, much higher ceilings on potential returns on investment.

However, there’s a flip side to excitement – greater uncertainty. Because of their relative youth, digital coins don’t have enough track records of financial success and stability. As a result, you can consider them as highly speculative investments.

Part of the insufficient track record of financial viability is that mainstream businesses haven’t adopted cryptos as an alternative payment option until now. Given that they were created to provide consumers and businesses alternative ways to transact, they still haven’t made significant progress in terms of being a viable payment mechanism.

Hence, most cryptocurrencies are highly speculative investments. Even the most established coins like Bitcoin, Litecoin, and Ethereum still leave much to be desired in terms of mainstream acceptance as an acceptable payment method.

I just want to clarify that I’m not implying cryptocurrencies will surely fade away within the next 5 to 10 years. I’m just saying that as a general species of investment assets, a huge part of the growth potential and excitement surrounding them is great uncertainty. That’s why you must be wise when investing in cryptocurrencies.

Also, you can minimize your cryptocurrency investing risks by going for the “blue chip” tokens like Bitcoin, Litecoin, Ripple, Ethereum, and Dogecoin. The first four are considered crypto pioneers. Dogecoin has been around for a while, and its market price has surged to record highs over the last few months. It also doesn’t hurt that no other than Elon Musk has endorsed Dogecoin recently, regardless of how shallow his reasons may be. Given the pioneering statuses of these coins, it’s reasonable to expect less volatility or uncertainty from them.

Specifically for Bitcoin, the granddaddy of all digital tokens, the fact that futures contracts and options with it as underlying assets are trading on the CME and CBOE is a solid indicator of increasing public confidence in it. Even if it’s still not as widely accepted as an alternative payment method, it has become a widely accepted – and coveted – investment instrument in mainstream finance.

Lack of Regulatory Oversight

Double-edged characteristics seem to be the norm for cryptocurrencies. This time, it’s the coins’ autonomy.

Lack of regulatory oversight isn’t really that big of a concern. But still, you’ll need to consider this when investing in this type of asset. There are a couple of reasons to do so.

First, most global financial regulators are not too enthusiastic about digital coins, to say the least. And you can’t blame them because if you were given the legal and constitutional mandate to protect your country’s citizens against financial scams, I doubt you’ll embrace a very popular investment vehicle you have no jurisdiction over. It doesn’t help that such autonomy gives money launderers a lucrative haven to invest in the spoils of their criminal activities.

Hence, given that autonomy is one of the cryptos’ most important and non-negotiable features, the monetary authorities’ acceptance of them as legal tender is still highly unlikely in the near term.

Another reason to consider autonomy before investing in this asset class is that you don’t have any legal protection or recourse against unauthorized transactions without regulatory oversight. If hackers successfully steal your digital coins, you can’t run to the Federal or State government for legal action. You’re on your own!

Contrast this with traditional investments like stocks, bonds, or businesses. Through a variety of agencies, Federal and State governments can penalize and compel those who aggrieve you to compensate you and return what they stole. It’s because you know who you’re dealing with, and they’re under US and State laws, too.

However, you don’t need to let this concern stop you from investing in digital coins. You can employ the same investing workaround strategies to mitigate or minimize risks associated with lack of regulatory oversight: limit your investment amount to what you’re comfortable losing only. By doing this, you can start investing in cryptocurrencies without feeling anxious about this specific type of risk.

I think it’s worth noting that the lack of regulatory authority also contributes to the very high volatility in the market price of cryptos. Lack of regulatory authority leads to higher investment risks and, therefore, higher volatility.

The regulatory autonomy also puts investments under this asset class at risk of being targeted by governments, whose announcements have the potential to impact crypto market prices strongly. Take, for example, the Chinese government and Bitcoin.

A couple of years ago, Chinese monetary authorities hinted at plans of prohibiting crowdfunding activities meant to raise funds for new digital coin offerings; average market prices of Bitcoin all over the world dropped by as much as 20% within a few trading hours only. Even though such a potential regulatory change in China won’t impact Bitcoin, as it is already a publicly-traded and established cryptocurrency.

Weigh the Pros & Cons Carefully

At this point, you already have an idea of the potential investing issues you’ll face if you decide to push through with investing in cryptocurrencies. Carefully evaluate them vis-a-vis your personality, preferences, priorities, and goals. Only after doing that should, you decide regarding cryptocurrency investing. That way, you can maximize your chances of either successfully investing in digital coins or avoiding unnecessary stresses.

Should you decide to pursue cryptocurrency investing, turn the page and continue reading the remaining chapters. If not, I would still recommend finishing reading this book. At the very least, you’ll have a much clearer idea of how to invest in this type of asset. Who knows, maybe you’ll change your mind by the time you finish the book.

Chapter 4

 

Investment Security:
Your Cryptocurrency Wallet

Since you’re reading this chapter, I think it’s safe to assume you’ve made up your mind to start investing in cryptos at the soonest possible time. To help you successfully do that, I’ll first help you become familiar with what will undoubtedly become your single biggest asset for buying and selling digital coins: your wallet!

No, I’m not talking about the kind you put in your back pocket or purse. What I’m talking about here is your digital wallet.

This is important because cryptos are digital assets, i.e., they have no physical versions. They’re also decentralized and autonomous, which makes choosing the right kind of wallet more crucial for securing your investments against hacking. The lack of regulatory oversight means you can’t afford to leave them vulnerable to online theft, and the right crypto wallet can help you ensure the security of your precious digital investments.

The best place to start understanding and appreciating the importance of cryptocurrency wallets is by learning the underlying transaction process. Just like buying and selling stocks, derivatives, and US Treasuries are done on financial exchanges, trading cryptos are done on cryptocurrency exchanges.

When you purchase a cryptocurrency on an exchange, you are assigned a unique digital code corresponding to your transaction amount. This digital code is more popularly known as a public key, and it’s uniquely specific to your cryptocurrency transaction amount only.

Remember the blockchain? This is one of the most important pieces of information that is lodged as part of your official cryptocurrency transaction records in the blockchain, together with the corresponding transaction amount.

Crypto exchanges won’t just assign public keys for your transactions’ entry into the relevant blockchains. The exchange on which you purchase cryptos will also assign private keys to your transactions, which you must guard with your life. Why?

Your private keys are the only means by which you can access the digital coins you purchased or invested in. Regardless of if you lose your private keys due to forgetfulness or theft, it doesn’t matter because you’ll suffer the same consequence: permanent loss of your cryptocurrency. Unlike your email address or online bank accounts that provide ways for you to access them in the event that you lost or forgot your password, cryptocurrency exchanges offer no such thing. Hence, make sure you never forget it and that you guard it with your life.

At this point, you may be wondering what it’s got to do with having to pick the right crypto wallet? If you entrust your private keys and the corresponding cryptocurrencies to the exchange where you bought them from, you put your private keys and digital coins at high risk for theft. Regardless of how sincere cryptocurrency exchanges are in securing your cryptos, that’s not a guarantee that your investments are safe and sound. You need to move them out of the exchange and into a safe storage place that’s inaccessible to hackers. That place is your cryptocurrency wallet, and as the name suggests, it’s the digital version of your physical wallet because it’s where you can safely store your investments.

There are two kinds of crypto wallets: hot and cold. How are they different?

The difference lies in Internet connection. Hot wallets are basically online wallets. On the other hand, cold wallets are offline ones.

Hot wallets are your standard or default option. It’s because this is what cryptocurrency exchanges typically offer their customers. However, the point of using a digital wallet is to ensure your private keys are inaccessible to hackers. By the nature of exchanges being online all the time, the risk of getting hacked and stolen exists, regardless of how good your exchange’s security systems are.

Another variation of a hot storage wallet are apps installed on computers used for regular Internet use. Compared to your exchanges’ hot wallet, this one has a much lower risk of being hacked simply because you have complete control over your private keys and wallet encryption.

However, this type of hot storage wallet still puts your cryptos at risk of getting lost, either via theft or amnesia. Although you can control when to go online and off, hackers still have a chance of being able to hack your computer successfully and steal your private keys.

Even if you find ways to minimize your computer’s hacking risks, there’s still a chance you might lose your crypto investments if you store them in a hot storage wallet. If your computer becomes irreparably damaged, you’ll no longer be able to access your cryptos even if you have your private keys.

Given these risks, your best bet for safely storing your cryptocurrencies is a cold storage wallet. One of the reasons for this is obvious: hackers can’t hack something that’s offline! While you’ll need to go online when you eventually decide to sell or liquidate your investments, you can severely limit your wallet’s online exposure by immediately going offline as soon as your transaction’s done. With app-based hot storage wallets installed on your computer or smartphone, your wallets are online for as long as you’re using your device to surf the net, even if you quit the app.

So, how do cold storage wallets look like? They’re small electronic storage gadgets that you can plug and unplug from your computer, typically using its USB port. You can safely store your private keys in a cold storage wallet because you’re only going to plug them into your computer when you need to make a transaction. Otherwise, you can just keep it disconnected or unplugged from your computer, so there’s no physical way for hackers to access your private keys even if your device is still connected to the Internet for other things you need to do.

The only caveat to using a cold storage wallet is cost. With very strict cryptocurrency security comes hefty price tags. But all things considered, what’s the one-time cost compared to the peace of mind this type of wallet can give you all throughout your cryptocurrency investing journey?

If you’re seriously considering investing in a cold storage wallet first, what are your options? While there are many in the market, some of the most highly-rated and trusted ones include:

  1. Ledger Nano X.
  2. Trezor Model T.
  3. Ledger Nano S.
  4. Exodus; and
  5. Electrum.

Every time you move your digital coins from your crypto exchange account to your hardware or cold storage wallet, you also move your private keys. As explained earlier, cold storage wallets are offline storage devices. This means hackers won’t be able to steal your cryptos. Obviously, nobody can hack something that’s offline. The only thing you’ll need to ensure is access to your wallet and keys are limited to only you (or one other person you know you can trust with your life).

You’ll also need to create a backup document – preferably a physically printed one – for your private keys. If you forget them, you won’t be able to access them in your cold storage wallet for liquidation or transfer to another account.

Finally, there’s another associated cost to using a cold storage wallet: transaction fees. Every time you move digital coins to and from your exchange account and wallet, you’ll have to pay a relatively small fee that’s typically determined as a percentage of the cryptocurrency amount involved.

Why do you need to pay fees? Remember that miners need to solve complex algorithms or mathematical equations to validate cryptocurrency transactions, including yours. They aren’t exactly non-profit organizations that provide pro-bono work. Because they need to be compensated, typically in the same cryptocurrency they’re mining, you’ll have to pay a small fee for being able to move or transact your digital coins.

Another reason why fees are imposed on cryptocurrency movements is to keep the blockchain from becoming flooded with minor or insignificant transactions. Even if the crypto-mining field is full of willing and able miners, too many transactions may put so much strain on their limited computing powers. When inundated with a deluge of transactions, the average transaction validation time for a crypto’s blockchain may significantly increase and keep you waiting much longer than usual. Thus, the transaction fees are also meant to – hopefully – discourage voluminous and malicious transactions that increase the risk of clogged networks.

Chapter 5

 

Important Cryptocurrency
Investing Mindsets

The fact that you’re still reading this means you really are serious about investing in digital coins. In this case, there’s another important thing you’ll need to learn about before embarking on your journey: mindsets.

What are mindsets? They refer to a set of deeply-held beliefs, values, and convictions about oneself and the world. Mindsets are responsible for the results you consistently get in your life, including:

  1. Abundance or lack.
  2. Satisfaction or discontent.
  3. Joy or regrets; and
  4. Health or sickness.

Why are mindsets so important when it comes to investing? It’s because they determine your habits or automatic actions. For example:

  1. If you truly believe that money grows on trees, you can easily and confidently grab opportunities for new and high-risk investments.
  2. If your mindset is that money is hard to earn, you’ll likely be a very conservative investor to the point you won’t make any high-ROI investments due to risk aversion.
  3. If you have a growth mindset, you’re probably a habitual learner, so you can continue growing in your investment skills and be able to take advantage of new opportunities like cryptocurrencies; and
  4. If you have a limited or fixed mindset, you wouldn’t take the time to study and learn new kinds of investments and investing techniques and fail to take advantage of new opportunities.

Let’s look at some of the most important mindsets for maximizing your chances of successfully investing in cryptocurrencies.

Your Why

Other than wanting to make more money or maximize investment returns, have you ever considered your deeper reason for wanting to invest in cryptocurrencies? Why do I ask?

It’s because making more moolah or achieving optimal ROIs is usually just a means by which to achieve something more meaningful. These “somethings” may include long-term financial goals like ensuring you can afford to send your children to a good private school near you or to be able to enjoy life more by having enough money to retire early at 50 years old, among others.

What’s the significance of knowing your compelling why for investing in cryptocurrencies? If your “why” is strong or compelling enough, you can count on it to power you through very rough patches in your investing journey that make you feel like quitting.

If you don’t have a powerful enough reason, the temptation to quit when the market turns bad or once the excitement fades can be strong enough for you to give in and cut your cryptocurrency journey short. Having a compelling “why” will likely keep you from quitting.

Another reason to be cognizant of your compelling why is enabling easier decision-making. When you’re clear about your priorities and why you’re investing in digital coins, you’ll probably avoid analysis paralysis.

Let’s say your reason for investing in cryptocurrencies is to maximize the returns on the funds you’re setting aside for early retirement. In that case, it’ll be easy for you to choose between Bitcoin and some obscure newbie token that’s becoming more popular by the day. If you prioritize a middle-ground crypto investment, i.e., lower risk but with lower potential returns, you’ll lose no sleep over choosing Bitcoin. By lower, I mean in the context of high-return assets like cryptocurrencies that are still higher than most market investments.  But if you value maximum returns regardless of the risk, then choosing the new and obscure one should be very easy.

Remember, you’ll make much wiser crypto investing decisions with greater ease when you’re clear about your strong why. On the other hand, not being cognizant of your reason or having a flimsy one increases your risks for making unwise investing decisions.

Your Required Rate of Return

Before you start picking your cryptocurrency investments, you must determine your required or expected rate of return. Why?

It’s all about the risk-return principle, i.e., the higher the expected return, the higher the investment risk you must be willing to take. Be clear on the required rate of return on your investments to achieve your financial goals. That way, you’ll have an easier time short-listing potential digital coin investments to those that have a history or high potential to meet your minimum expected ROI, maximum risk you’re willing to tolerate, or both.

To better understand how to use your minimum expected return to shortlist your investment options, let’s use an already publicly available cryptocurrency’s average annual rate of return and volatility.

For illustrative purposes only, let’s assume that Dogecoin’s average monthly market return is 15% and its standard deviation, a common measure of volatility, is 20%. Let’s also assume that your minimum required monthly ROI is 13%.

At first glance, Dogecoin’s average monthly return qualifies it to be included in your shortlist of possible crypto investments. After all, 15% is 2% higher than your minimum requirement of 13%. Right?

As measured by the standard deviation, Wrong Volatility will tell you by how much the expected monthly returns of Dogecoin will likely be above and below the average. In other words, using standard deviation can give you a reasonable expectation – based on historical performance – of the lowest and highest possible expected return.

Using our assumed figures (again, for illustrative purposes only), we’ll deduct the standard deviation from the average monthly return to estimate the lowest expected return. In this case, 15% (average monthly return) minus 20% (standard deviation) equals -5%, which is a loss. This means the worst expected scenario with Dogecoin is a 5% loss for the following month.

Next, we’ll add the 20% standard deviation to the 15% average monthly ROI. This yields a possible maximum expected return of 35% for next month.

Overall, you can expect your ROI for next month to be between a 5% loss or a 35% gain. Since the minimum expected return based on volatility is below your minimum required, Dogecoin shouldn’t be among your shortlisted cryptos for investment consideration.

The bottom line is this: without identifying your minimum required rate of return (regardless of if weekly, monthly, or annual), you won’t have a clear and objective basis for choosing cryptocurrencies wisely.

Your Timeline

More than just your required rate of return, another thing that should be clear in your mind is your investment timeline. Why is this important, too?

The answer lies in the time value of money, particularly the principle of compounding. When it comes to investing, compounding identifies four key principles that will determine how much your investment will grow over time:

  1. Investment amount.
  2. Growth rate.
  3. Compounding frequency or how many times will you reinvest the gains in each period; and
  4. Time or duration.

Given all things equal, a higher investment amount will result in a higher future value of an investment. The same holds true for growth rate and compounding frequency, i.e., the higher the value, the greater the returns.

When it comes to time, the longer your investment period is, the greater your investment’s future value can become. But if you want to achieve the same future value of an investment with a shorter investment period, you’ll need to do any or all the following:

  1. Increase your investment amount.
  2. Invest the same amount at a higher growth rate; and
  3. Increase the compounding frequency.

Therefore, it’s important to start saving and investing for retirement as early as possible. The later you start, the more money you’ll need to invest, the riskier you’ll have to invest (for higher returns), or both!

When you know how much time you need to achieve your investment goals, it’ll be easier for you to determine your minimum required ROI. If you don’t, you run the risk of unsuccessfully investing for your financial goals.

If you don’t want to take on excessively higher risks than you’re comfortable with, consider a longer investment horizon. That way, you won’t be pressured to take on digital coins with volatilities that exceed your risk tolerance levels.

And speaking of time horizons, many expert fund managers gradually reduce their investments’ exposures to high-risk assets towards the end of their investment horizon. Why?

This is to minimize the risk of huge losses too close to the investing finish line. If a high-risk investment’s market value plunges severely close to your retirement and wipes out a huge chunk of value from your retirement investment fund, two bad things can happen:

  1. You won’t have enough time to recoup the huge losses, leaving you with insufficient retirement funds; or
  2. You’ll be forced to take on equally higher or even riskier assets for potentially high returns to compensate for the short remaining investment time.

As any professional fund manager worth their salt will tell you, when it comes to long-term investments: invest more aggressively in the beginning and taper off as you get close to the end of your investment period. You can take on higher risks in the beginning (and enjoy potentially higher returns) because in case things go south, you’ll have enough time to recoup losses. Towards the end of your investment horizon, it’s best to start locking up more of the substantial gains you’ve already made to minimize the risks of failing to achieve your goals due to substantial late-period losses on your investment.

Your Risk Tolerance

All investments have risks. The only thing up for debate is what kinds of risk you can tolerate and how much.

There are different kinds of risks associated with investments. These include:

  1. Market risk refers to the possibility of losing money due to unfavorable changes in your cryptocurrencies or other market-driven financial assets.
  2. Liquidity risk or the possibility of either not being able to liquidate your investments fast enough or liquidating them at a lower price than what you want to sell them for; and
  3. Credit risk is the possibility that your counterparty won’t be able to deliver the underlying security or payment for your transaction.

To help you determine your risk tolerance, ask yourself these questions:

  1. How much money are you comfortable losing (market risk)?
  2. What’s the longest you can commit a specific sum of money for investing in cryptocurrencies (liquidity risk)?

Keep in mind that cryptocurrencies, in general, are high-market risk investments. You must limit your investment to an amount that you’re comfortable losing and won’t need in the next 1 or 2 years. Otherwise, you might end up with anxiety attacks when your digital coins’ market values plunge at some point in time.

Also, remember to consider the average trading volume and price differentials of cryptocurrencies before investing in them to help you manage your liquidity risks better. If you invest in a cryptocurrency with a very low trading volume, it means transactions are very slow and that it might take you a while to liquidate your investment when you need money. Cryptocurrencies with relatively large gaps between the highest and lowest bid and ask prices, respectively, increases the risk for delayed liquidation, lower selling price than what you want, or both. Unless you have a relatively long investment horizon, it’s best to stick to those with relatively high average daily trading volumes.

Profit-Taking & Stop-Loss Triggers

Believe me when I say that market-driven investments like cryptocurrencies and stocks offer so much profit potential that it can be very easy to let emotions take over. Whether it is greed that’s keeping you from locking in on your investment profits/gains or fear of realizing actual losses, emotions can drive you to invest unwisely.

Let me share my experience with emotional investing with you, i.e., not using profit-taking & stop-loss limits. Many years ago, I invested in a stock whose market price, fortunately, gained 35% in just one month. Afterward, negative information about the company’s future earnings came out. Considering the gravity of the information, the stock’s price understandably dropped by 10% in the next five trading days, cutting my ROI to just 25%.

Ruled by my overly positive outlook on the stock despite the recently available information, I was sure that the drop was just a correction. One month later, I lost 10% of my investment.

Had I been clear on my profit-taking and stop-loss limits, I could’ve locked in on superior returns on that stock. Unfortunately, greed and fear got the better of me, resulting in a 10% loss on my investment. From gold to trash, that’s where emotional investing took me.

Remember, a bird in your hand is better than one in the bushes. Don’t be too greedy but instead, be a realistically positive and objective investor. Besides, no one can predict prices at which market-driven securities will peak or bottom out.

Chapter 6

 

Investing in Cryptocurrencies

Now that we’ve got the important preparatory aspects of cryptocurrency investing down, let’s start talking about what you need to do to invest successfully in this kind of financial asset. Again, may I remind you that digital coins are very risky investments compared to traditional bonds and stocks. But it’s this volatility that will provide opportunities to earn very high returns that may exceed even those from the stock market.

Trading vs. “Investing”

You probably have heard many investors talk about why trading is better than investing or vice-versa. Based on such conversations, you’d think they’re two different financial activities.

If you did, you’re both right and wrong. I didn’t mean to confuse you, but these two terms can mean the same and different things, depending on the context of your perspective.

Let’s start with what do investors usually mean when they contrast trading and investing activities. Trading refers to investing activities with a short position closing period. Typically, trading means buying and selling financial assets on exchanges within the same day. Suppose the position is short-selling, i.e., selling assets you don’t have yet. In that case, trading typically means selling early during the trading day and covering the short position, i.e., buy shares from the market to deliver to your earlier buying counterparty before the end of the trading day.

Opening and closing trading positions within the same day is referred to as day-trading.

These days, trading has evolved in terms of shorter and longer trading periods. Because of advances in computing power, many traders execute shorter and shorter trades. From taking and closing positions anytime within the trading day, sophisticated traders can now open and close positions within the morning or afternoon, within the hour, or even within minutes or seconds.

Trading positions can also take longer to close without being disqualified as “trading.” It can be as long as several days, weeks, or even months.

By contrast, “investing” refers to longer position-taking investment activities. Typically, positions held for at least a year are considered investing. Other popular references for this strategy include long-term investing, buy-and-hold, and buy-it-and-forget-it.

If the context of the comparison is the position holding period, trading and investing are two vastly different things. But in general, they’re the same thing: they’re financial investments.

Investing refers to the act of committing financial resources, e.g., money, to a specific activity or financial asset for a specific time period with the intention of generating a meaningful return on such commitments. In other words, to invest means to commit or put cash in a specific financial asset for a specific time frame in order to make that money grow substantially.

In that context, both trading and long-term investing are the same banana, albeit two different-sized ones. Trading is like a small Orito or Lady Finger banana while investing is like a long cavendish one. The objective is the same: to make money grow. How the objective can be accomplished is also the same: committing money to financial assets that will either provide income, increase in market value, or both. Hence, they’re both classified as investments.

Which of the two strategies should you adopt? There’s no standard answer because they have their own pros and cons, that will determine their suitability as your investing strategy.

Let’s look at trading first. This will require close monitoring of investments for timely execution of buying and selling transactions. Because trading operates on short investment time frames, small price movements can spell the difference between profitable trades and losing ones.

Another aspect of trading is frequency. To make substantial profits in short periods of time, you’ll need to take and close positions on digital coins multiple times within a short period, e.g., hour, day, week, etc.

If you don’t have enough time for close monitoring and frequent position taking/closing daily, trading won’t be a good fit for you. That’s why this isn’t the appropriate investment strategy for people who are constantly busy throughout the day.

Another aspect of a trading investment strategy is higher transaction costs. Particularly if you trade on an exchange and use a cold storage wallet, you’ll have to pay relatively small transaction fees every time you move digital coins to and from your accounts. While these may be relatively small, frequent trading can lead to higher total transaction costs at the end of the day or period.

On the other hand, long-term investing is the preferred choice for most investors, whether it’s stocks or cryptocurrencies. One of the primary reasons is simplicity.

Think about it: buy a cryptocurrency or two today and just hold on to it/them for at least a few months. While holding on, you don’t have to monitor market prices every day or every hour. Depending on how long your investment horizon is, you can even afford to update yourself on current market prices once or twice a month. The point of checking on market prices is to just stay on top of how your investment’s doing. Unless developments are dire and have the serious potential to compromise the long-term value of your investments, you don’t have to take frequent action on your digital coin holdings.

Another benefit of it being a simpler investment strategy is less stress. With trading, you need to always be on your toes because, given the very short investment horizon, even small price movements can result in losses. Short-term volatility can also result in frequent “whiplashes” or false signals either way, which can cause investment losses to pile up very quickly. This can lead to upward spiraling stress levels.

But in terms of profitability, neither has the advantage over the other. It all depends on how good your timings are, especially for trading and market price movements.

At the end of the day, trading may be appropriate for you if you have a lot of free time on your hands daily, you’re an adrenaline junkie, and if you have nerves of steel. But if you want your cryptocurrency investing to be as simple as possible, you’re chill, and have a long-term investment horizon; long-term investing may be more appropriate for you.

Welcome to the Jungle: Your First Crypto Investment

The first step in your cryptocurrency journey is choosing the coin to invest in. While it’s tempting to think that all cryptos are practically the same and just have different creators and names, that’s not really the case. Each digital coin has its own unique qualities that help differentiate them from one another.

Thus, it would be a big mistake to start your digital coin investing journey with such a mentality. You’ll need to research well about the major cryptocurrencies to kick things off.

After you’ve chosen a specific cryptocurrency to invest in, you’ll need to choose the platform on which you’ll make the investment. Basically, it means choosing your cryptocurrency exchange, signing up for an account, and buying the digital coin you chose.

Just a refresher, exchanges refer to businesses or institutions that facilitate buying and selling of financial assets. Different exchanges carry different financial assets. The New York Stock Exchange (NYSE), for example, facilitates the buying and selling of shares of stocks of American and foreign companies. If futures contracts and options are your things, the appropriate exchanges are the Chicago Mercantile Exchange and Chicago Board Options Exchange, respectively. For buying and selling digital coins, cryptocurrency exchanges are the place to be.

One of the reasons why you’ll need to sign up for an account with at least one cryptocurrency exchange platform is because there’s nowhere else to buy and sell digital coins. Unlike shares of stock that can be bought over-the-counter (outside the platform), cryptocurrencies are digital assets that can only be traded on online exchanges. You really have no choice on this matter except for selecting a specific exchange.

To help you choose where to sign up for an account, here are some of the most popular and biggest crypto exchanges you can consider joining based on Forbes.com’s article on the subject that was updated last 27 May 2021:

Binance.US, voted as the best overall cryptocurrency exchange and the best crypto enthusiasts.

  1. Coinbase, which the site voted as the best cryptocurrency exchange for beginners.
  2. Kraken.
  3. Crypto.com.
  4. Gemini.
  5. Gate.io.
  6. Kuoni.
  7. Bitstamp.
  8. Bittrex; and
  9. BitFlyer.

Once you’ve chosen your platform, proceed with opening an account on it. One thing you’ll need to know before doing so is that opening accounts with cryptocurrency exchanges tends to be more tedious compared to exchanges of other types of financial assets. This is because cryptocurrencies and their blockchains aren’t under any regulatory oversight (autonomy, remember?). Thus, they need to take extra steps to really ensure you are who you’re claiming to be.

Still, the relatively tedious account opening process can only work in your favor. It guarantees your account’s security and privacy. Who wouldn’t want that, eh?

After you’ve successfully opened an account, the exchange you opened an account with will likely ask you for valid identification documents. These may include scanned copies of valid government-issued IDs like a driver’s license or your passport.

The waiting period for having your account application approved and validated shouldn’t take long. On average, it takes anywhere from 3 working days to over a week to finish.

Finally, you’ll need to buy cold storage hardware for securely storing your digital coin investments. Remember why I recommended buying a cold storage wallet? That’s right because it’s an offline storage device.

However, cold storage wallets do come at a relatively higher cost. So, if your budget is limited, you can always settle for hot storage wallets. However, they have higher risks of getting hacked, given they’re typically connected to the Internet.

To minimize hacking risks, install your wallet on a device or computer that you can dedicate exclusively to cryptocurrency investing. By doing this, you minimize its online times by connecting to the Internet only when you have to buy or sell your cryptocurrencies. Other than that, you should keep it offline to ensure hackers won’t be able to access it and steal your private keys.

Regardless of your choice of wallet, you should immediately move your cryptos, including your private keys, from your exchange account to your wallet. This ensures your precious investments won’t be at risk of getting hacked on the platform. In recent years, there have been several exchange hacks that resulted in millions of dollars’ worth of digital coins being stolen. These include:

  1. Mt Gox in 2014 ($600 million)
  2. Coincheck in 2018 ($534 million).
  3. Bitgrail in 2019 ($195 million).
  4. Coinrail in 2018 ($37.2 million).
  5. Bithumb in 2019 ($30 million).

Hence, it’s not a good idea to keep your cryptos in an exchange. Transfer it immediately to your wallet as soon as you buy them. Move them back there only when you’re about to sell.

One of the things you’ll also need to consider about buying and selling digital coins on exchanges is doing so isn’t free. Exchanges charge fees for the privilege of trading on their platform, which is fair. Otherwise, how would they continue operating and provide investors like you with a reliable crypto trading platform over the long term?

Don’t worry – transaction fees typically don’t cost an arm and a leg. Most exchanges charge a fixed percentage of transaction values. Still, this is one of the things you should consider when choosing your crypto exchange.

Before we go to investing strategies, allow me to summarize the process of investing in cryptocurrencies as a beginner:

  1. Pick a cryptocurrency to invest in.
  2. Pick the cryptocurrency exchange on which you will trade.
  3. Once your cryptocurrency exchange account has been approved and validated, choose the kind of wallet where you will store your cryptocurrencies and their corresponding private keys. The most preferable is a cold storage wallet.
  4. Purchase your desired cryptocurrency using your newly opened and validated exchange account; and
  5. Transfer your cryptocurrencies and private keys to your chosen storage wallet immediately to ensure their safety and security.

Investing Strategies

As the saying goes, luck favors the prepared. If you want to be very “lucky” when investing in cryptos, then you’ll need to prepare well. One important preparation strategy is research.

Specifically, you should research the historical prices of major cryptocurrencies so you can compute the mean (average) and standard deviation (volatility) of their returns. If you’re looking to trade rather than invest, compute for the average daily returns and standard deviation. If you’re looking to apply a long-term investment strategy, you can compute for the average monthly or annual rates of return and the corresponding periodic standard deviation.

If you remember from an earlier chapter, you’ll need these figures to determine the lower end of a cryptocurrency’s average periodic rates of return. When you subtract the standard deviation from the mean periodic return, you’ll get the most conservative (lowest) expected rate of return for the next period.

If a digital coin’s average monthly rate of return based on market price movements is 15%, and its standard deviation is 10%, the minimum expected return for the following month is 5%. If the standard deviation is 20% given the same average monthly ROI of 15%, then the lowest expected return for next month is -5%, i.e., a loss.

If the lowest expected ROI is higher than your minimum requirement, you can include it in your shortlist of possible digital coin investments. If it’s lower or if it’s negative, it’s disqualified.

You don’t need to learn how to compute the two statistical inference numbers manually. You can just use a spreadsheet such as Microsoft Excel or Apple’s Numbers to compute for it. You just need to download and paste historical prices on your spreadsheet. There are many easy-to-understand tutorials on the Internet on using spreadsheets to compute the mean and standard deviation automatically.

After you’ve computed the mean and standard deviation of the historical returns of select cryptos and have shortlisted them based on minimum expected return, it’s time to choose your cryptocurrencies. Take note that I wrote cryptocurrencies instead of just cryptocurrency. This leads us to the next important investing strategy: diversification.

When you spread your investment across two or more coins, you’re able to spread your investment risks and, in the process, minimize them. Allow me to illustrate.

You have $1,000 to invest in cryptocurrencies, and you chose to put it all on the hottest ticket at the moment: Dogecoin. If the price drops by 10% in 1 week, you’re down by $100 already.

If you diversified your investible funds equally between Dogecoin and Bitcoin, you invested $500 each on the two coins. Assuming Bitcoin’s price didn’t change while the same thing happened, i.e., Dogecoin’s price drops by 10%, your market loss would’ve been limited to just $50 (10% of $500). Overall, your portfolio’s marked-to-market loss is only 5%, i.e.:

Cryptocurrency’s Percentage Share of the Portfolio X Percentage Gain/(Loss)

50% x 10%

5%

Of course, this is a highly simplified example, and that Bitcoin’s price is unlikely to remain static. Now, let’s see how the diversification dynamic works when both prices change.

Let’s say Bitcoin’s price dropped by 15% together with the 10% drop in Dogecoin. Because of diversification, your portfolio’s overall market loss would be less than 15%.

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = [Crypto A % Gain/(Loss) x % of Portfolio] + [Crypto B % Gain/(Loss) x % of Portfolio]

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = [-15% x 50%] + [-10% x 50%]

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = -7.5% + -5%

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = -12.5%

As you can see, diversification can help you lower your investments’ financial risks. However, this works both ways. While it can lower your portfolio’s overall loss on investments, it can also reduce the overall gain. What do I mean by this?

Let’s reverse the examples given earlier. Instead of a 15% and 10% market loss on Bitcoin and Dogecoin, respectively, let’s make them market gains instead.

Had you invested solely in Bitcoin, you would have registered a 15% gain for the entire portfolio. But by diversifying your portfolio equally among Bitcoin and Dogecoin, your overall market gain would be less than 15%. Using the same formula from earlier:

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = [15% x 50%] + [10% x 50%]

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = 7.5% + 5%

Portfolio % Gain/(Loss) = 12.5%

If diversification gives your portfolio the benefit of letting lower-risk items in your portfolio mitigate higher risk from the others, it can also work the other way. The lower ROI items may also mitigate the higher returns on the others and result in a middle-of-the-ground return on the total portfolio.

This isn’t exactly a bad thing. A positive way of looking at it is to look at the difference between a diversified portfolio and one that focused solely on Dogecoin. Had you put the entire $1000 on that cryptocurrency, and it registered a 10% increase in market value during the first week, your entire portfolio’s ROI would only be 10%.

But if you diversified your investable funds equally between that and Bitcoin, the latter’s higher ROI for the same period (15%) can pull up the portfolio’s total return to 12.5%. So, it’s simply a matter of choosing to look at the glass of water as either half full or half empty.

However, keep in mind that diversification is primarily about risk mitigation. As such, you shouldn’t focus or mind the possibility of a lower total portfolio ROI compared to that of the highest return investment in your collection. If you let greed become your primary guide when it comes to choosing your investments and constructing your portfolios, you’ll end up with a portfolio that has excessively high risk. Thus, you may end up losing a lot of money that may be more than what you bargained for.

And if you remember my story earlier, being greedy can turn your treasures into nothing. That’s why it’s important to shortlist your cryptocurrencies wisely using historical market returns and standard deviations. Between lower-than-expected returns and huge losses, I’m sure you’ll agree that the former is much more desirable.

Cost Averaging

The formula for making money in market-driven financial assets such as cryptocurrencies is this: buy low, sell high. The opposite, buying high and selling low, is only applicable to 2 instances: trading of fixed income securities, where buying and selling prices are expressed in terms of yield to maturity, and liquidity is desperately needed.

This formula is most effective in bullish or upward-trending markets. When market prices are generally going up, your chances of buying at a relatively low price and selling at higher ones are very high.

This is the primary reason why most investors scramble to buy market-driven securities during established bull runs. And when you think about it, it makes perfect sense. After all, why would you consider buying cryptocurrencies, stocks, and the like when markets are bearish? After all, isn’t the point of investing to make money, and how can you buy low and sell high when prices are on a downtrend?

Fortunately, downward trending markets also provide opportunities for increasing returns on total investments. If you want to take advantage of such opportunities, you need to learn a strategy called cost averaging.

This is a strategy used to bring down the average cost of a specific investment. With lower average costs, the break-even price for a particular investment will also go down. In the end, it can lead to a higher ROI for that asset.

Using a cost averaging strategy, you’ll continue buying a specific market-driven financial asset at lower prices. If you’re familiar with the concept of the statistical mean or average, it’s easy to understand how buying more units of the same asset at lower prices will lead to a lower average cost or price.

I know it can sound confusing, so let me illustrate with numbers. Let’s say you invested half of your $1000 investable funds in Dogecoin at $0.35 per coin. This would give you 1,428.57 units of said crypto.

If the market price of dogecoin drops to only $0.30 per unit, this translates to a loss of $0.05 per unit or a $71.43 total market loss for the portfolio. As a percentage of your total $500 investment, this translates to a 14.29% loss.

For you to break even again, you’ll have to wait for the prices of dogecoin to go back up to $0.35 cents per coin. Remember, this is the price at which you initially bought your dogecoins. This doesn’t even consider transaction fees, which will increase your actual break-even price. But just to simplify this illustration of how cost averaging works, we’ll just consider the buying and selling prices.

Now, what if you bought another 1428.57 units of the cryptocurrency at $0.30 per unit? It will bring down the average buying cost of your dogecoin portfolio to only $0.325 cents per unit. How did that happen?

Buying another 1428.57 units at $0.35 per coin gives you a total of 2857.14 dogecoins. Your two purchases of $500 for the first batch and for $428.57 for the second give you a total investment cost of $928.57 for Dogecoin.

Now, this is where the magic of cost averaging comes in. If you divide $928.57 by 2,857.14 total dogecoin units you now have, you’ll get a cost of $0.325 per unit of the crypto. By purchasing more dogecoins at the lower price of $0.30, you were able to bring down your average buying price to only $0.325.

How does this benefit you? For one thing, it helps you break even much faster. In this example, you had to wait for the market price to go back up to $0.35 per token in order to break even. But when you doubled your dogecoin holdings by purchasing another 1428.57 units at $0.30 per coin, you brought your break-even market price down to the lower average buying price of just 32.5 cents. If you simply want to break even, you’ll just need to wait for its price to retrace half of the original $0.05 drop, i.e., $0.025.

The other way you can benefit from this is by increasing the total ROI on your Dogecoin investment. Instead of waiting for the market price to exceed your original cost of $0.35 per token, we just need to wait for it to exceed 32.5 cents. And if you were patient enough to liquidate everything at $0.35 per unit, you would have made a three-cent profit on each token for an ROI of 7.69% instead of just breaking even.

Many seasoned investors manage their risks through cost averaging or not putting all their investable funds into a specific market-driven asset in one hit. Unless it’s very clear that market prices are about to skyrocket, they spread out investing in a specific security over a period of time regardless of whether the market is trending downward or upward.

Just as with diversification; cost averaging can work both ways. Cost averaging in an upward trending market can lead to a lower ROI for a specific cryptocurrency because it’ll drive the average purchase cost upward. As a result, the break-even selling price will also go up and, in the process, reduce the ROI.

But just as in the case of a diversified portfolio, this isn’t really a profit-maximizing strategy but a risk reduction one. Your average buying cost for a particular token will only go up through cost averaging when its price is on a bullish trend. As such, the market price will continue exceeding your average buying cost for that specific crypto and ensure your investing or trading position is always in the black.

Alternative Investments in Cryptocurrencies

What if you’d like to enjoy the high ROI benefits of digital tokens but aren’t too keen on investing in them directly? Fortunately, this is possible because there are alternative investment instruments that can allow you to do that.

One of them is managed funds or trusts that invest in cryptocurrencies. Here, a fund management company pulls together funds from a myriad number of investors – including you - and invests them in digital coins. If this sounds awfully familiar, it is. These are no different from mutual funds and hedge funds that manage investors’ funds on behalf of clients.

When you invest in a cryptocurrency-oriented fund or trust, you enjoy the benefit of passively investing in digital tokens. You can rest assured that your cryptocurrency investment is in good hands because professional fund managers actively manage your and other people’s collective investments in digital tokens practically 24/7. You neither have to fret over closely monitoring market prices and developments nor ensure timely execution of transactions. They’ll do all the work for you.

Just a caveat, though, they don’t do it for free. Depending on the fund management company, fees can be based on your investments’ market value or their performance for the year. Make sure to read the terms and conditions carefully and compare the fees of at least two different funds or trusts.

An example of a cryptocurrency investment fund is Grayscale. At the end of 2020, it was the biggest cryptocurrency fund in the world, with managed funds totaling over $10 billion in market value. Established in 2013, Grayscale initially focused on Bitcoin and was first called the Grayscale Bitcoin Investment Trust. Over the years, it has added more digital tokens to its portfolio and is now known simply as Grayscale to emphasize its newly diversified investment lineup.

When you invest in managed funds, how do you make money? Just as with directly investing in digital coins, you make money when your investments’ net asset value or NAV goes up.
